# Vision and plan

**Current interpretation, 9 October 2026:** read [the curled-torus measurement scope](docs/papers/measurement_scope.md)
before reusing the historical claims below. Square counts do not certify geometry or a unique
front; `bath_T` is mean store energy; the seed scan measures escape; the persistence edge is
operational. O108 and the methods supplement give the evidence. Original dated predictions,
scores and failed tests remain on record; their stronger interpretations are superseded.

This page is the fixed reference. Changes to it are deliberate commits that say why.
Source keys refer to `REFERENCES.bib`; assumption numbers refer to `ASSUMPTIONS.md`.

## The hypothesis (author's, in plain words)

1. Our spacetime is a stable phase of something deeper, called X. X is itself a specific, relatively stable arrangement, with its own degrees of freedom, its own dimensions and its own scale. It is not a hot, random or structureless state. *(Second and third sentences added by the author, 2026-09-20; see "What X is" below.)*
2. We know this phase's rules well: general relativity, special relativity, quantum mechanics. Any candidate for X must end up reproducing them. *(This is the standard bar for every quantum-gravity approach; none has cleared it.)*
3. X and our spacetime are two arrangements of **one underlying system with one underlying relational quantity, F**, which says which arrangements are favoured. F need not be energy as we know it. In X it is a measure of relational pattern (closeness or connectedness among degrees of freedom, possibly built from several such measures for different classes of degrees of freedom). Energy in the familiar sense exists only in the spacetime phase, where differences in F show up as energy and mass. *(This is how the models already work: the "energy" of a graph is a count of loops, a purely relational number with no units; [T25] Introduction argues that energy, length and speed are all emergent and only a dimensionless coupling is fundamental, and its Eq. (45) converts a free-energy difference of the network into curvature and mass.)* What claims 4–6 need is only that F is a single comparable number across X and spacetime, so that "more stable", "released" and "conserved" have a meaning. They share a base, but their working descriptions need not resemble each other: between X and spacetime, degrees of freedom may merge (many act as one), split (one acts as many), convert type, freeze (hidden dimensions), or be left over (the remainder in claim 5). X is the more symmetric, higher-energy arrangement: higher in F than what it turns into, which is spacetime, together with X2 if X2 exists *(last clause added 2026-09-20)*. *(Precedent for type conversion inside this model family: link variables reorganising into photon-like gauge fields, [KMS06] Sec. IV.)* The count at the base level may be fixed or may vary. What matters is that **one energy function covers every configuration**, including configurations with different counts (precedent: dynamical triangulations, where the number of building blocks varies and the cost per block plays the role of the cosmological constant; Ambjørn, Jurkiewicz and Loll, arXiv:hep-th/0105267, not read by us, from general knowledge). Claims 4–6 lose their definitions only in models that have *no* energy function and are defined purely by a growth rule (e.g. causal-set sequential growth; Rideout and Sorkin, arXiv:gr-qc/9904062, not read by us). Steps 2–3 use a fixed count because the published model does; a varying count is parked. *(Corrected 2026-09-19: an earlier version wrongly said a varying count by itself removes the meaning of claims 4–6.)*
4. A phase change in X produced our phase. It worked the way a chemical reaction does: X was stable for the time being but was not the most stable arrangement available; the one less stable part of it gave way (collapsed or expanded), and the whole rearranged into something more stable. The change is collective: it starts at a seed and spreads outward as a front. The number of large dimensions may be different before and after (a dimensional phase change). ~~It was **first order**: it~~ It came with **a jump in energy**: it released a bounded lump of energy (Δρ) that became matter and radiation. *(Second to fourth sentences added by the author, 2026-09-20. Last sentence reworded 2026-10-11 at the author's decision, Update 56: "first order" is withdrawn from the claim. Her words: "I don't care whether it's first order. I'm just interested in if there is a jump in energy at some sizes we are measuring." The testable part is the jump.)*
5. What did not convert remained as hotter X, or snapped into another discrete arrangement, X2. *(Added 2026-09-20, the author's words put in order. There are two possibilities. If X2 exists, it is a product of the change, as a reaction can have two products, and spacetime and X2 together are lower in F than the X they came from. If X2 does not exist, the remainder is still X: its state may change (hotter, for example), but it has not changed phase.)* **Broadened later the same day, at the author's decision: the leftover may be more stable than spacetime or less, and it may be an allotrope in [T24]'s sense, dark energy, or X2. What is required is only that the change happens, that it releases energy able to become the Big Bang's energy and the large geometric dimensions, and that the leftover accounts for the rest of the books. The direction of the leftover's own stability is not part of the claim.**
6. Across the transition an energy-like quantity is conserved, which is what makes "energy released" meaningful. After spacetime exists and expands there is no global energy bookkeeping, as in standard general relativity.

Claims 1–6 accepted by the author on 2026-09-19. The additions of 2026-09-20 to claims 1, 3, 4 and 5 are the author's own words from conversation, put in order by the assistant; merging the change that introduced them is her acceptance.

Status: a hobbyist's hypothesis. Items 4–6 are untested ideas. Nothing here has been reviewed by a physicist.

### What X is (author's, added 2026-09-20)

**Why this was added.** While explaining a chart, the assistant described the hot side of the simulated model as "the random tangle, X". The author corrected it: nothing in claims 1–6 says X is random, and claim 3 already called X "the more symmetric, higher-energy arrangement". The mistake was to treat the hot phase of one published model as if it were X. What follows for the model and the plan is in Update 5 below.

**The order of events, stated so that nobody has to ask.** This was written the day after the exploratory runs of Update 4 showed no first-order sign at N = 160. It does not move the bar: S1–S5, the knob λ, the gates and the plan are as they were, and the bad news of Update 4 stands as written. What it changes is how much a result in this one model can be said to show about claim 4, and it changes that in both directions (Update 5).

**The analogy: a chemical reaction.**

| Chemical reaction | Here | Claim |
|---|---|---|
| A reagent that is stable for now, but is not the most stable arrangement on offer | X | 1, 3 |
| Activation energy: it needs a push to get started | The barrier to forming the first seed of the new arrangement | 4 |
| Energy released per molecule | Energy released per unit converted: the latent heat, Δρ | 4 |
| Products, plus reagent that did not react | Spacetime, and X2 if it exists, plus X left over (still X, perhaps in a changed state) | 5 |
| Energy balances across the reaction | The conserved energy-like quantity | 6 |

One real difference: a reaction goes molecule by molecule, each on its own, while a phase change is collective. A flame is the bridge between the two: a reaction that spreads as a front. *(From general knowledge, not read by us: the growth of bubbles in early-universe phase transitions is treated with the mathematics of combustion fronts.)*

**An illustration, not a claim.** One arrangement X might have, in the author's words put in order: ~~four large space dimensions, and~~ a time dimension that is tiny. In the change, time becomes large~~, and the fourth large space dimension collapses and loops in on itself, leaving a bud of spacetime with three large space dimensions and one large time dimension~~. *Revised 2026-09-22 at the author's decision (Update 13): X has **fewer** large dimensions than spacetime, not more. It is curled, and the change uncurls; a tiny direction becoming large is that, and the collapsing fourth dimension is withdrawn. In the author's words of the same day, "the tube opens".* *(Annotated 2026-10-19, consistency audit: what the toy has shown for this is narrowed in Update 54 (O116); since 9 October the toy's stand-in for X is the curled slab, two directions open and one curled, not the tube (Updates 47, 58, 60, 64).)* Any relatively stable arrangement and set of degrees of freedom could play the part of X; claims 1–6 do not depend on this one. Its specifics (why 3+1, hidden dimensions) stay on the parked list.

**Each element of the illustration has a published ancestor**, and a physicist reading this page (S5) will ask about them. *(All from general knowledge; none read by us. Bibliographic details checked against the Crossref catalogue on 2026-09-20.)*

- A state that is stable for now converts by a seed and a spreading front: [C77].
- The energy released by such a change becomes all the matter and radiation: [G81], the original form of cosmic inflation. **It failed**, for a stated reason: the bubbles of new phase never merged into one smooth universe, because the space between them grew faster than they did [GW83]. *Ours, unverified:* whether this hypothesis inherits that failure is open. The failure needs the old phase to be an expanding space, and X need not be one.
- A space dimension curled into a small loop: Kaluza and Klein, 1920s (textbook). A looped dimension collapsing to nothing through a seed and a spreading front: [W82].
- Why exactly three space dimensions grow large: one proposed mechanism is [BV89].
- A bud of new space pinching off from a parent: [BGG87].
- The number of dimensions differing between the phases of one model: causal dynamical triangulations (Ambjørn, Jurkiewicz and Loll, as cited in claim 3), and the model used here, whose random phase has infinite Hausdorff dimension while its geometric phase has dimension close to 2 ([T25], text around Eq. (24) and Fig. 6; read by us).

## What success means (author's bar)

An existence demonstration: at least one model in which the mechanism of claims 3–6 demonstrably happens, and which nobody qualified has shown to be wrong. The conditions are fixed here, before any run, so that the bar cannot move afterwards.

- **S1 No cherry-picking.** We may explore a *family* of energy functions, but only along knobs fixed in advance, and we publish the whole map: every setting run, including those that give the answer we did not want. A single member of the family is never presented on its own. *(Reworded 2026-09-19 from "No tuning", after agreeing that the useful question is which ingredients force a first-order transition.)*
- **S2 First order.** At the largest sizes we can reach, the energy histogram at the transition has two humps and the valley between them deepens as N grows; heating and cooling runs show hysteresis. *(Annotated 2026-10-19, consistency audit: since Update 56 S2 is the bar for the published question only, not one the hypothesis sets for itself; claim 4 now says "a jump in energy", and the hypothesis's own route is scored by S2′.)*
- **S2′ Sharp change between two orders** *(added 2026-09-22, the author's decision; Update 13)*. S2 is written for a transition driven by temperature. A change from one specific arrangement to another under a fixed rule is a decay with an activation cost — the reaction of the analogy under "What X is" — and there is no temperature sweep in which it could show two humps, so S2 cannot be applied to it. For that case the bar is: (i) both arrangements are dips of F (every single move out costs) at every size tried; (ii) the energy gap per point between them is fixed and does not shrink with N; (iii) the change proceeds by a seed and a front, with the two orders coexisting on either side of it and almost nothing in between; (iv) the activation cost does not grow with N; (v) sealed, the leftover is fixed by the energy released and survives. **Said plainly: (i) to (iv) were met by T7 and Update 9 before this condition was written, so it is a bar written after those runs, and they must not be counted as having cleared a bar that did not exist when they ran. What it makes falsifiable from here is (v), which T9 tests, and whether the leftover grows with the space, which T10 pre-registers.** S2 stays as written for the temperature-driven case.
- **S3 Sealed split.** At fixed total energy the system separates into an ordered region plus a hotter remainder, and the energy released per converted node is measured.
- **S4 On the spot.** The ordered region passes the geometry and stability lines of the target below.
- **S5 Someone qualified has looked.** "Nobody has proved it wrong" only counts if a physicist has actually read it. Silence from people who never saw it is not evidence.

Precedent: [CP12] already reports a first-order transition with a nucleation barrier into a nearly two-dimensional phase with defects, so S2 has been met once in a related model. S3 and S4 have not been done in any model we have found.

If S2 fails in Trugenberger's model, that is a result too: the hypothesis does not live there, and the write-up says so.

## The working question

**Which ingredients of F force two dips (first order, latent heat), and which remove them (continuous)?**

*(Annotated 2026-10-19, consistency audit: since Update 56 whether the change is first order is no longer part of the claim; this question stays as the published question's, and the hypothesis asks for a jump in energy at the sizes measured.)*

The mathematics of "requiring two humps": if φ is the order parameter (squares per node), the probability of finding the system at φ is P(φ) ∝ exp(−N·f(φ)/g), where f is the cost per node including entropy. Two humps in P means two dips in f. The smallest f with two dips is f = aφ² − bφ⁴ + cφ⁶ with b > 0, the same potential as in the author's original 10-step plan; with the opposite sign of the φ⁴ term there is one dip and the transition is continuous. The changeover is a tricritical point. Because N multiplies the exponent, a small barrier per node becomes an overwhelming one in a large system, which is why the valley deepens with N. *(Landau theory; textbook material, e.g. Chaikin and Lubensky, not re-read by us.)*

Building a model that must give two humps and then finding two humps would prove nothing. Mapping which ingredients decide the order is a real result.

**First knob, fixed in advance: λ, the strength of the local term.** H = H_global + λ·H_local, with H_global and H_local as in [T25] Eq. (22).

- λ = 0: square count only. Published: first order, graph shatters into isolated hypercubes ([T25]; [KTB19]; [GV21]).
- λ = 1: the full Ollivier curvature. Published: continuous, a surface forms ([T25]).
- 0 < λ < 1: not a curvature any more, only an interpolation between the two. Unexplored as far as we know. The question is where the order changes, and whether any λ gives first order *and* a connected space (conditions S2 and S4 together).

Why the local term is there at all: it was not added as a design choice. It is what the Ollivier curvature does by itself. That curvature compares the cost of moving one node's neighbours onto the other's; once an edge carries 2D−2 squares every neighbour already has a partner one step away, so further squares cannot lower the cost and the curvature saturates at zero (the [·]₊ brackets of [T25] Eq. (8)). "Global plus local" is bookkeeping for "linear reward, then saturation" ([T25] Eqs. (9), (10), (22)). Ollivier curvature was chosen because it is the one graph curvature proven to converge to the Ricci curvature of general relativity ([T25], section on convergence). So in this family the ingredient that removes the two dips is the same ingredient that makes the energy faithful to general relativity.

**Preferred measurement: the entropy curve.** Instead of histograms at one temperature, measure s(φ), the log of the number of arrangements at each φ, directly (Wang–Landau sampling [WL01]). A dent (a stretch where s curves upward) means first order; the dent's width is the latent heat and its depth the barrier. No dent means continuous. One measurement covers all temperatures, and it is the natural quantity for the sealed, fixed-total picture of claim 6.

## The root question

**Does geometry form through a first-order transition with a latent heat, or a continuous one?**

Items 4 and 5 stand or fall on it. First order → a latent heat exists, so Δρ has a home. Continuous → no latent heat, and the rival picture below is the one the models support. *(Annotated 2026-10-19, consistency audit: no longer so since Update 56: claim 4 says "a jump in energy", first order is not part of the claim, and this is the published question's root question, not the hypothesis's.)*

## How this differs from Trugenberger's combinatorial quantum gravity

Shared starting point: spacetime is a phase of graph-like degrees of freedom [Trugenberger23; KT19].

| Question | Trugenberger | This hypothesis |
|---|---|---|
| Order of the transition | Continuous; the critical point *is* the Big Bang and supplies the continuum limit [Trugenberger23 Sec. III] *(Annotated 2026-10-23, Update 94: the Big Bang is a difference between the two pictures, not a shared premise. In his model it is effective, an appearance to an internal observer of a static network; in hers it is a literal event)* | ~~First order, with latent heat~~ A jump in energy: a bounded lump released (reworded 2026-10-11, Update 56; whether the change is first order is no longer part of the claim) |
| Where matter comes from | Matter *is* leftover bubbles of the random phase; its energy is excess curvature [Trugenberger23 Sec. I] | Matter comes from the released energy; the leftover is a separate remainder (hot X or X2). *Decided 2026-09-23 (Update 19): the leftover is a structured defect of fixed size, never the melt* |
| What drives expansion | Geometry alone: negative curvature behaves like de Sitter space [T22; T23]. Since 2024 he also describes an inflation-like burst at emergence ("topological inflation"), caused by very high curvature near the critical point, not by released energy [T24, Dark energy section] | Released energy (inflation-like epoch, then reheating) |
| Time | An absolute universal time that is not geometric; the time of relativity emerges later, at large scales [T23] | Same choice, and the same split. **Corrected 2026-09-21 by the author: time is not created by the change.** X already has something time-like about it — an ordering, or a time-like direction arranged differently from ours — and the change *rearranges* it, exactly as it rearranges the spatial directions. That is claim 3 applied to time rather than to space. What emerges is the time of relativity, the one that mixes with space and runs at different rates; the bare ordering is assumed on both sides and derived by neither. Her illustration of 2026-09-20 already said this: X has a tiny time-like direction that *becomes* large. ~~our time is emergent~~ was the loose phrasing and is withdrawn |
| Dark sector | Dark energy = leftover curvature of the ground state. Dark matter = long-lived metastable "allotropes": regions of spacetime stuck in a different discrete arrangement (fewer squares per vertex), like diamond surviving where graphite is stable [T24] | X2: a discrete alternative arrangement that gravitates. **Close to his allotropes.** ~~Difference: his are *less* stable than space and left behind; X2 was proposed as *more* stable than X.~~ *No longer a difference (2026-09-20): claim 5 now allows the leftover to be more stable or less, so his allotropes are a candidate instance of X2 rather than a rival to it.* |
| What changes over cosmic history | The coupling runs; more and more matter turns into spacetime [Trugenberger23 Sec. I] | A sealed system with a conserved energy-like quantity |
| Entropy problem | Rescale the coupling with system size, giving an area law [KTB19 Sec. 3.1.1] | X has structure (bounded partners), so entropy is extensive (ASSUMPTIONS B1, C4) |

The two pictures make **opposite predictions about one measurable thing**, the order of the transition. The published evidence for "continuous" is one correlation-length plot at N ≈ 200, which its authors call a "reasonable indication" in 2D and say falls short of a finite-size-scaling analysis [KTB19 Sec. 4–5]. A simplified version of the same model (local terms dropped) is first order with hysteresis, per a second group [GV21, abstract only]. So the question is open, and it discriminates.

Update 2026-09-19: [T22], [T23] and [T24] have now been read in full. What they add:

- He still takes the transition to be continuous, but says so conditionally: he proceeds "assuming that its nature can be further cemented by large-scale numerical simulations presently out of reach of the available computational resources" [T22, Introduction]. His largest graphs are N = 2000 in 2D and N = 500 in 3D [T22]. So the test in step 3 is one he has named himself.
- [T24] contains no simulations ("no datasets were generated"). The allotrope picture of dark matter is an argument, not yet a numerical result.
- *Ours, unverified:* long-lived metastable domains and a free-energy minimum that switches between discrete levels (1, 2, 3, 4 squares per vertex) as the coupling falls are the usual signature of **first-order** behaviour, yet [T24] attributes them to a continuous transition. If those switches are real, each would be a first-order step with its own latent heat. That gives Δρ a possible home even if the first transition (random to geometric) is continuous.
- He states that general relativity has not yet been derived in the large-scale region [T23], and that numerical work has only been possible in 2D [T23, footnote].
- The programme is now built on the **2D** model: a negatively curved surface acting as a holographic screen, with 3+1 appearing as the large-scale random-walk dimension [T23; T24]. So D = 2 is the physically relevant case in his own terms, and it is the cheap one to simulate.

Update 2 (2026-09-19): [T25], a full review published December 2025 (arXiv version April 2026), was pointed out by the author and read in full. What it adds:

- **The order of the transition depends on one term of the energy.** Global term only: first order, but the graph breaks into isolated hypercubes, not a space. Full Hamiltonian (with the local penalty): continuous, and a surface forms [T25, Cycle condensation]. So a first-order version of this model exists, but it does not produce geometry. For this hypothesis that is a tension between success conditions S2 (first order) and S4 (on the spot) inside this model family, and it has to be faced, not avoided.
- Our first-look simulation used a hard cap instead of the local penalty, so it matches neither published case (ASSUMPTIONS Q3). Step 2 continues: implement the full Hamiltonian, reproduce [T25] Fig. 3 at N = 160.
- Black holes in [T25] are large bubbles that have reverted to the random phase. That overlaps the parked idea of black holes as phase changes.
- Einstein's equations are obtained there as constitutive relations by a uniqueness argument, "still at a qualitative level" in the paper's own words.
- The heaviest published runs (N = 2000) are reported to need months of CPU time per size.

Update 3 (2026-09-19): at the author's request the text of [T25], [KTB19] and [T22] was searched directly, not summarised, to settle open points, and the capped code was then run once at the published size. Why this page changes: Update 2 says our first look "matches neither published case". That was wrong, and the knob below has a third published point. Earlier text is left as written. Details, quotations and numbers: `ASSUMPTIONS.md`, section D and O5.

- **The capped model is a published case.** [KTB19] Sec. 4 states that its simulations (Figs. 8 and 9, the evidence offered for a continuous transition) used the mean-field action on the space where no edge carries more than d − 2 squares. That is our code. [KTB19] did not simulate the annealed transition for the full Hamiltonian; it says only that the same universality class "seems likely".
- **So the first knob has three published points, not two** (*Ours*): λ = 0, global term only, first order, hypercubes; λ = 1, the full Ollivier curvature of [T25]; λ → ∞, the hard cap of [KTB19] Sec. 4, since an infinite penalty is a prohibition. The knob and its direction are unchanged; its range is now 0 ≤ λ ≤ ∞, and the cap is its far end, not a mistake to be removed.
- **Our readings of the model were checked and hold**: the energy, including its coefficient ([T25] Eqs. (8), (21)), and the hard-core rule ([T25] Fig. 1b). The published random-phase floor 0.126 is a formula, not a measurement.
- **The published N-independence of the D = 2 critical coupling is an argument, not a measurement.** It follows from rescaling the coupling by |V|^(1−2/D) so that the continuum action stays finite ([KTB19] Sec. 3.1.1; [T22]), which is no rescaling at D = 2. [KTB19] calls a finite-size-scaling analysis "impractical in the present model", and its Fig. 8a shows curves for N = 100 to 200 that do not collapse, in the direction of the drift we see.
- **Exploratory, unverified, and it bears on the working question.** At N = 160 the capped code matches the digitised [T25] Fig. 3 to about 0.01 across the hot side, and the rise begins in the same place, g ≈ 6.3. Below it the published curve jumps by about 0.45 within 7 % in g, while ours, which is in equilibrium there (opposite starting states agree to 0.001), rises smoothly, as does the capped-model figure of [KTB19]. If Fig. 3 shows the uncapped model, which is what the text of [T25] discusses, then the published record already holds the contrast this project asks about: **a cap gives a smooth crossover, a soft penalty gives a sharp jump, with the onset in the same place.** [T25] reads that jump, with no hysteresis at N = 160, as continuous. At that size a weak first-order transition would look the same: the barrier between the two phases grows with N, so at small N a system hops across it freely and shows no hysteresis. Telling the two apart is what conditions S2 and the entropy curve s(φ) are for. None of this is a finding until T2 reproduces the jump.

Update 4 (2026-09-19): T2 was built and the test named in Update 3 was run the same day. Why this page changes: the last bullet of Update 3 offered an explanation, the test refuted it, and a page that is the fixed reference must not keep a refuted explanation standing unmarked. Update 3 is left as written. Details: `ASSUMPTIONS.md`, section D.

- **The explanation in Update 3 is withdrawn.** λ = 1 without the cap does not produce the jump of [T25] Fig. 3. It gives a smooth curve, in equilibrium (opposite starting states agree to 0.003), about 0.04 above the capped one.
- **The kernel reproduces [KTB19] Fig. 8a**, the curve named as the gate of plan step 2: rms difference 0.005 over the 14 digitised points at N = 160 where our chain equilibrates. So the sampler and the energy are right to half a per cent against an independent published curve. (The text of [KTB19] says that figure used the cap; its numbers fit the uncapped model three times better. Unexplained.)
- **The disagreement is therefore between two published figures**, same group, same N = 160: 0.62 at g = 5 in [KTB19] Fig. 8a, 0.99 in [T25] Fig. 3. The papers do not contain what is needed to resolve it. It is a question for the authors, and now a precise one.
- **For the hypothesis this is not good news, and it is recorded as plainly as the good news was.** At N = 160 neither published variant we can build shows any first-order sign: no jump, no hysteresis, no growth of fluctuations. Both look like the continuous transition the literature claims. The only published figure that looks first order is the one we cannot reproduce. What remains open is what S2 always required: larger sizes and the entropy curve, since a weak first-order transition and a continuous one cannot be told apart at N = 160. And λ = 0, where first order is the published result, is the next test (Gate A).

Update 5 (2026-09-20): the author clarified what X is (see "What X is" near the top). Why this page changes: the updates above say "the random phase", which is correct, but the assistant's explanations had begun to call that phase X, and what our results can be said to show about claim 4 depends on the difference. Earlier text is left as written.

- **The model's hot phase is not X.** In this model the hot side is a random graph: "a locally tree-like random graph" [T22, Sec. II, after Eq. (3)], with infinite Hausdorff dimension [T25, text around Eq. (24)]. The system sits there because there are overwhelmingly many such graphs, not because it is a particular arrangement that F favours. By the author's definition it is a stand-in for X, and a poor one. From now on the project's text says "the random phase" or "the model's hot phase", never X.
- **What steps 2 and 3 test is therefore narrower than claim 4**: whether geometry forms *out of a random phase* through a first-order transition. That is still the published open question, still the point on which this hypothesis and Trugenberger's make opposite predictions, and still the next thing to do. The author confirmed the same day that reproducing his published results comes first: it is respectful to a predecessor, and it shows that we understand the mathematics. Gates A and B, the knob and S1–S5 are unchanged.
- **This cuts both ways, and the second way is the inconvenient one.** A smooth result in the random-to-geometric transition would mean what the line under S5 already says, that the hypothesis does not live there; it would not show that a specific X cannot convert first order. Equally, nothing simulated in this repository so far contains an arrangement that fits the author's definition of X. So nothing here yet tests claim 4 in her sense, and a first-order result at λ = 0 (Gate A) would also be a change out of a random phase.
- **Where this model family does hold changes between two specific arrangements.** (a) The ladder of discrete levels in [T24] (1, 2, 3, 4 squares per vertex), already in plan step 3. (b) The knob itself. Written edge by edge the energy is H = 4 Σ_e [(2 − S_e) + λ (S_e − 2)₊], where S_e is the number of squares on edge e and the most an edge can carry is 3 (*Ours*: a rearrangement of H = 16(N − S) + 4λX using S = ¼ Σ_e S_e and 2N edges; the limit of 3 is stated in [T25], same passage as the tie; both are checked on a melted graph by `test_energy_edge_by_edge`). So below λ = 1 the lowest energy belongs to graphs with 3 squares on every edge, of which isolated 4-cubes are the published example (−8(1 − λ) per vertex); above λ = 1 it belongs to graphs with exactly 2 on every edge, the flat sheet (0); and at λ = 1 they tie. [T25] states the tie in words (the two effects "cancel exactly", denser configurations are "degenerate"; ASSUMPTIONS Q3), and `tests/test_cqg.py::test_four_cube_energies` pins the numbers at λ = 0, 0.5 and 1. So at low coupling, crossing λ = 1 swaps the favoured arrangement between two specific ones: closed 16-vertex objects and an open two-dimensional sheet. No new knob is needed to look at this, which matters for S1. What is needed is a sampler that does not freeze at low coupling (T5). It stays behind the gates.
- **Does a change between two specific arrangements have to be first order?** *Ours, unverified; Landau theory, textbook, not re-read by us.* Only in one case. If neither arrangement's symmetry is a reduced version of the other's (graphite and diamond are the usual example), a continuous change is ruled out, so it is first order. If one is a reduced version of the other, both orders are possible and the details of F decide (the sign of the φ⁴ term in "The working question"). The author's illustration is of this second kind: four equal large dimensions, of which one is singled out. So the clarified picture makes first order natural, not guaranteed, and it still has to be measured.

Update 6 (2026-09-20): first look at the λ = 0 end of the knob with our own kernel (task T3 done; exploratory runs; details in `ASSUMPTIONS.md`, Q8 and section D). Why this page changes: Update 4 recorded that neither variant we could build showed any first-order sign. One now does, and it is the one the literature says should.

- **At λ = 0 the published behaviour appears.** On cooling, φ jumps between two neighbouring couplings; on heating, the cold state survives to a coupling about 2 higher; inside that loop every replica sits in one of two states about 0.85 apart in φ (about 13.6 in H per vertex), with nothing between; on the hot side of it the two legs agree to 0.005. Three sizes, four replicas. At λ = 1 and under the cap the same kernel shows none of this.
- **And, as published, the cold phase is not a space.** The graph shatters into about N/16 closed pieces. So S2 and S4 pull against each other inside this model family in our own runs too: the end of the knob that has two states has no space, and the end that has a space has, so far, one state. Whether any λ between gives both is still the question of step 3.
- **For claim 4 this is a possibility shown, no more.** It shows that a graph energy of this family can produce two coexisting states with a definite energy gap, which is what a latent heat is. It does not show it for a transition that ends in geometry, and per Update 5 none of these transitions starts from anything like X.
- **A small finding of our own.** The published ground-state pieces at λ = 0 are described as hypercubic. There is a second one, a 14-vertex graph (the points and blocks of the 7-point biplane), which ties with the 4-cube in energy at every λ and forms by itself in about one finished piece in seven. *Ours, checked by computer, unreviewed.* Worth a line in the note to the authors.
- **Not claimed:** that the transition at λ = 0 is first order. Hysteresis shows two long-lived states; S2 asks for the histogram or the entropy curve, which is T6.
- *Added the same day:* the owner decided that these runs pass Gate A (`TASKS.md`), the reproduction check for λ = 0. Gate B, the one for λ = 1, is still open.

Update 7 (2026-09-20): the author asked to work towards designing a model of X itself: one set of degrees of freedom that can turn into a less symmetrical, space-like set. Why this page changes: that goal was implied by claims 1 and 3 to 5 but was nowhere in the plan, and three exact facts found while answering her bear on the working question. The requirements, the factors at play between two phases, and candidate directions are in `docs/design/model_x_brief.md`. Merging this update is the author's acceptance of plan step 5 below; it changes nothing in steps 2 to 4.

- **Dimension, in this model, is a count at each vertex.** Four edges make six pairs; a pair that closes no square is a direction that stays large. Flat sheet: two large dimensions, 1 square per vertex. Tube (one side curled to length 4): one, 1.25. 4-cube (both curled): none, 1.5. Energy per vertex is −4(1 − λ) for each curled side. So the author's illustration, a dimension that collapses and loops in, exists in the code as a curled side, and a dimensional phase change is a step on this ladder. *(Exact; `test_sheet_tube_cube_ladder`.)*
- **Below λ = 1 the family has the mechanism of claim 4, wired backwards.** Sheet and cubes are both dips of the energy (every single move out costs energy), and the cubes are the lower one. The state that is stable for now and would convert with a release of energy, 8(1 − λ) per vertex, is the space-like one. *(Exact; `test_which_arrangements_are_dips`.)*
- **Between λ = 1 and λ = 2 it has it the right way round, in miniature.** Exhaustive search at N = 18 finds specific, connected arrangements that are dips above the flat ground state at λ = 1.25 and 1.5, and none at λ = 2 or 3 (`results/dip_census_small.csv`). Eighteen vertices are not a space and the walls are low; this says where on the knob to look, no more. Its large-scale relative is the allotrope of [T24]. *Added the same day, after the author asked what lies between λ = 2 and very large:* a finer scan (`results/dip_census_fine.csv`) puts the window at 1 < λ < 1.6 for N = 18, and above it nothing new happens: folded states just lose weight, and by λ of about 5 to 10 the model behaves as the capped one does (`scripts/exact_small_averages.py`). So the knob has three stretches, not two: folding pays (below 1), folding loses but folded dips survive (1 to about 1.6 at N = 18), and a smooth fade into the cap.

Update 8 (2026-09-20): parallel tempering was built and checked (T5), and the design track produced its first dynamical results. Why this page changes: Update 7 said the mechanism of claim 4 existed the right way round "in miniature" as a static fact about dips at 18 vertices. It has now been seen to *happen* in a run, and the page should say exactly what was seen and what was not. Details: `ASSUMPTIONS.md` section D; figures in `docs/figures/`.

- **The wrong way round, as a rehearsal (0 < λ < 1).** A perfect flat sheet sits at exactly zero energy for hundreds to thousands of sweeps, then drops abruptly. Its first step is one dimension curling up; every plateau is an exact mixture of the rungs of the ladder of Update 7, so the energy given off per converted vertex could be checked against numbers known in advance (5.92 to 5.96 against 6 in the fullest conversion). Too warm, and the sheet melts into the random phase and takes energy up.
- **The right way round (λ = 1.25).** A perfect tube, one large dimension and exactly 1 per vertex above the flat sheet, lasts thousands of sweeps at low coupling, then opens out within a few hundred sweeps into one connected, perfectly flat sheet, giving off exactly that 1 per vertex. Four runs each at 64 and 96 vertices and three couplings; at λ = 1.5 the tube does not last at all, which matches the exact census at 18 vertices.
- **What this is for the hypothesis.** An existence demonstration, at toy size, of the *shape* of claim 4 inside a published model and without a new knob: a specific, stable-for-now arrangement with fewer large dimensions, a threshold, an abrupt rearrangement, a definite energy given off, and a connected flat space-like arrangement at the end. It is the first thing in this project that points towards the hypothesis and not merely past it.
- **What it is not.** Not a phase transition in the sense S2 needs (nothing is known about how it behaves as N grows); not at sizes that can be called a space; the tube is long-lived but is not a strict dip; nothing says how a tube would come to be there; and at fixed temperature the energy given off is carried away, so claims 5 and 6 are untouched. Exploratory, not pre-registered, unreviewed.
- **Claims 4 and 5 may be one claim seen from two ends** (*ours, unverified*, prompted by the author's question of why one would choose between claim 4 and [T25] Sec. VI.1, where matter *is* the leftover random phase). Sealed, a conversion cannot reach the perfect ordered state: the energy it gives off has nowhere to go and leaves scattered disorder behind, φ = 0.95 rather than 1.00 in our runs. Scattered disorder inside a geometric network is precisely his definition of matter. So the latent heat of claim 4 would be *what manufactures* the leftover random phase of claim 5, rather than a rival to it, and claim 4 gains the concrete answer it lacked to "what is matter made of". This changes nothing about what must be measured: leftover bubbles are compatible with either order of transition and a latent heat is not, so the order still discriminates. It does mean that a sealed treatment is not an optional extra to claims 5 and 6 but the setting in which claim 4 becomes concrete.
- **T5.** Tempering reproduces exact averages at 18 vertices and, at N = 160 under the cap, extends the range where our curve can be trusted from g ≥ 5 down to about 3. The curve is smooth throughout, steepest and slowest near g = 2.85; that is where T6 should look for that model. It remains far from the jump of [T25] Fig. 3.

Update 10 (2026-09-20): claim 5 broadened by the author. Why this page changes: the comparison table above listed the direction of X2's stability as a difference from [T24]'s allotropes, and the author has decided that the direction is not part of her claim. Earlier text is left as written and struck through where it is now wrong.

- **What the claim now requires** is only that the change happens, that it releases energy able to become the Big Bang's energy and the large geometric dimensions, and that whatever did not convert accounts for the rest of the books. The leftover may be more stable than spacetime or less; it may be an allotrope in his sense, dark energy, or X2.
- **What this buys.** His allotropes stop being a rival to X2 and become a candidate instance of it, which matters because they are the closest thing in the published literature to the idea, and because the allotrope ladder is already on the plan as something whose order we can measure (step 3). Together with Update 9's reading, the whole of his sections VI.1 and VI.3 now sits *inside* claims 4 and 5 rather than against them.
- **What it costs, said plainly.** Claim 5 now excludes very little: almost any remainder satisfies it. It has become bookkeeping rather than a prediction, and it should not be counted as evidence for anything if it is later satisfied. The falsifiable weight of the hypothesis rests where it always did, on claim 4 and success condition S2: is there a latent heat at all. Nothing in this update touches that.
- **One consequence worth naming** (*ours, unverified*). If a leftover is allowed to be *more* stable than spacetime, then spacetime is itself only metastable and would, given long enough, convert into it. That is not a defect of the idea: it is the standard false-vacuum picture, the same structure as [C77] and the basis of the mainstream question about whether our own vacuum is stable. It does mean the hypothesis then predicts that spacetime has a finite lifetime, which is a real statement and should be written down as one rather than arrived at by accident. *The author accepts this consequence (2026-09-20) and adds that the transition might be triggered by a size or a condition rather than by cooling; see Update 11, where that turns out to be something we have already measured.*

Update 11 (2026-09-20): where the published case for "continuous" actually rests, why nobody has tested it, and what that makes of the root question. Why this page changes: the root question below says the published evidence for a continuous transition is "one correlation-length plot at N ≈ 200, which its authors call a 'reasonable indication'". That was right, and it is now traced to the source and is stronger than it looked. Details and quotations: `ASSUMPTIONS.md`, section D.

- **The decisive test has not been attempted by anyone, and the published record says so.** [T25] supports its reading with no hysteresis in its Fig. 3 and with "several other diagnostics for a second order transition have been tested [29], with positive results". Reference [29] is [KTB19], which says of its own work that "at present even such an analysis is precluded" of finite-size scaling and that "we are some way off the asymptotic regime". Finite-size scaling is what separates a weak first-order transition from a continuous one. The 2025 review cites the 2019 paper without repeating its caveat.
- **We are better placed to do it than they were**, which was not true when this project started. Parallel tempering reaches the cold couplings a single chain could not (T5), and the exhaustive enumeration at N ≤ 18 gives an exact density of states that a Wang-Landau sampler must reproduce, a validation nobody in the published work had. T6 stops being "the next task" and becomes the point of the whole exercise.
- **Why the published work could not see a latent heat even if there were one** (*ours, unverified*). Both papers work at a fixed coupling, which assumes an unlimited reservoir. That choice is standard and usually harmless, but it is known to fail at exactly one place: a first-order transition, where a system at fixed total energy sits in the coexistence region with the released energy heating what is left. The canonical ensemble jumps across that region. So the standard setting is blind precisely where claims 4, 5 and 6 live, and the sealed machinery of ASSUMPTIONS Q12 is not an optional extra but the setting in which the question can be asked.
- **Claim 5 narrowed back into a prediction, and the prediction tested** (*ours*; the author asked whether anything can be excluded mathematically). At fixed total energy the final temperature is set by the energy released, and the disorder left behind is a determined function of it. So claim 5 tightens from "there is a leftover" to **the leftover is fixed by the energy released, with no freedom**. *Tested the same day and it holds:* the equilibrium curve at λ = 1.25 was measured first, the sealed runs' φ predicted from it and written into their config, and over sparks spanning total energies of 1.19 to 3.50 per vertex the measured φ falls from 0.966 to 0.811 where the prediction says 0.952 to 0.814, the three largest agreeing to 0.003 (`ASSUMPTIONS.md`, section D; `docs/figures/leftover_determined.png`). Claim 5 is no longer only bookkeeping, which repairs the breadth Update 10 introduced. What remains untested is the step that matters: whether a real transition releases a latent heat at all, which is T6.
- **A size-triggered change of phase is already in our data, and it runs the uncomfortable way** (the author asked whether spacetime might transition again at some size or condition). Task T9 records that the crossover coupling drifts with ln N. If that holds, a universe growing at a fixed coupling crosses the transition at a particular size. The direction is *out* of the geometric phase, not into a new one: this is the old entropy problem of [K08] and [CP12] in another guise, and the published remedies exist to prevent it. It is a real structure in the measurements rather than a speculation, and it is task T9.
- **What the hypothesis does not have to explain** (the author's point, accepted): why matter is stable. That is observed. It enters only as a constraint, that whatever we identify with matter must not evaporate on a short timescale, and it is cheap to check in the sealed runs rather than being a question of its own.

Update 12 (2026-09-21): a second knob, decided; and the tension of Update 4 restated on the right object. Two papers by other groups were read in full by the author, and both of our records of them were wrong (`REFERENCES.bib`; ASSUMPTIONS Q15). What follows are the consequences for this page.

- **The literature is not one group, and the two groups do not contradict each other.** [GV21] is Monte Carlo on real graphs, not the mean-field study we had recorded, and it reports a **first-order transition with clear hysteresis** at the penalty-off end, with the same finite-size criticism of the earlier work that this project arrived at independently. [T25] reports a continuous transition with the full rule. **Each has looked at one end of the lambda line, and nobody has run both energies in one code at matched sizes.** That is the gap T6 and T8 fill, and it is a sharper statement of it than "nobody has tested this".
- **DECISION: runs that test the hypothesis use interchangeable points, where affordable** (*the author's, dated here as S1 requires for a new knob*). Every run in this repository treats the points as individually named. The alternative treats them as interchangeable, as identical particles are. The argument for it is this page's own: if the relationships are the real thing and the layout is a convenience, then the **names are a convenience too**. The whole knob gets published either way, and the honest expectation is written down before the runs: symmetric arrangements gain weight, and a shattered state is about as symmetric as an arrangement gets, so this knob may well favour shattering and count against claim 4. Against that, in [DQM25]'s own model the interchangeable version has a transition where the named one has none, and [EK25] is cited for a first-order transition that exists only in the interchangeable version. **The question it actually raises, whether this changes the *order* of the transition, is open in both directions, which is why it is worth running.**
- **Which points are interchangeable is not ours to choose; the arrangement decides it** (*ours, unverified*). A point is distinguishable exactly to the extent that its relationships distinguish it. The renamings that leave every relationship intact are precisely the ones nothing can detect, so counting them (Q15) *derives* how much of an arrangement is genuinely interchangeable instead of assuming it. Partial interchangeability, which the author raised, is therefore not a third option between the two: it is what the correction already does in every case. Measured at N = 160: a shattered state is favoured by about 10^29 *(annotated 2026-10-19, consistency audit: 10^29.4 to one decimal, Q15)*, a perfect sheet by 320, **and a melted graph by exactly 1**. So for a low-symmetry state, which is what a universe with matter in it is, the two treatments agree; they differ most for the idealised arrangements that are easiest to calculate with and that nothing is ever in.
- **The S2-against-S4 tension of Update 4 is real but has been stated about the wrong object** (*ours, unverified; raised by the author*). "Where the transition is first order the cold phase is not a space; where it is a space the transition is smooth" is a statement about the **fully settled** state, meaning the ground state or a run cooled far past the transition. **Nothing is ever in that state.** A fully settled network is an empty flat sheet; a universe with matter in it is partly converted, which [T25] says outright, since for him the matter *is* the unconverted part. The object claims 4, 5 and 6 are about is the half-converted state, and **whether that contains a connected geometric region has never been looked at by anyone**, because at fixed temperature it does not exist to be looked at: no temperature setting yields a flask of thirty per cent ice. At fixed total energy it does exist. This turns an apparent impasse into task T11, and the machinery for it (Q12) is already built.

Update 9 (2026-09-20, the same day, after Update 8): a prediction of ours about the mechanism behind Update 8 was written down and then refuted, and sealed runs were built. Why this page changes: Update 8 offered a reading of *how* the tube gives way; the first test of that reading failed, and a page that is the fixed reference must not leave it standing. What Update 8 reports having seen is unchanged; what it guessed about the mechanism is not.

- **Resolved, in favour of the local wall** *(added after the Arrhenius measurement, same day; see the next bullet for what was wrong)*. Counting exactly: the cheapest way out of a tube costs 12 and the sampler offers exactly 3 such moves a sweep **at every size**, because a sweep is 2N attempts while the proposals go as N² and the good ones as N. So the waiting time should be 1/(3 exp(−12/g)) sweeps with nothing fitted. ~~Over ten conditions spanning an eighty-five-fold range of waiting times and two sizes, the mean of observed over predicted is 0.997.~~ *Corrected 2026-09-23: the detailed record (ASSUMPTIONS, "The wall measured against temperature") has twelve conditions over a ninety-fold range and two sizes, plain mean of observed over predicted 0.983, weighted mean 0.884 ± 0.060, every ratio between 0.71 and 1.62. The figures struck through came from an earlier reading and reached a draft letter before being caught.* The wall is local, its height is the single move, and a flat waiting time in sweeps is what that predicts rather than evidence against it.
- ~~**Not supported: the waiting time does not fall like 1/N.**~~ We predicted, before running, that if the tube gives way by one region going over a wall then a larger tube would convert sooner. At N = 64, 96, 144 and 192, twelve runs each, the mean waiting time is flat with size where the prediction has it fall threefold. *A correction, made the same day:* the first reading of these runs used a criterion that was itself proportional to N, which exaggerated the effect; it was reported at five standard errors and is 2.9 after correction, which with twelve runs is suggestive and not conclusive (`ASSUMPTIONS.md`, section D, where both readings are kept). The rest of Update 8 stands: every size ends in the same place, and 9 to 11 runs in 12 reach a *perfectly* flat sheet, so the energy given off per vertex is the exact 4(λ − 1) at every size tried.
- **The wall itself is local, and sharp.** Sealed, with the demon holding a fixed "spark" and no other energy anywhere, a tube converts if and only if the spark is at least 12 units: in 320 recorded runs nothing below 12 ever started a conversion and nothing at 12 or above ever failed to, at five sizes from N = 48 to 192 alike. Twelve is exactly the cheapest move out of the tube that the energy allows (losing 2 squares and 4 surplus squares: 32 − 20). So the cost of *starting* does not grow with the system while the energy given off, 1 per vertex, does. That is the condition for a runaway, and it points the opposite way from the waiting times. The two are not reconciled, and the mechanism is unknown. *Ours, unverified, exploratory.*
- **A sealed system with nothing to spare cannot convert at all.** With an empty demon the tube sits for ever, although the state it would convert to is lower: there is nothing to pay the wall with. The author's supercooled water says the same, and it is now a permanent test.
- **Energy is now conserved exactly when we want it to be** (`src/graphity/sealed.py`, ASSUMPTIONS Q12): H + demon + what has leaked never changes, to the last bit, and the temperature is read off the demon rather than imposed. That is the machinery claims 5 and 6 need. The leak rate, between sealed and wide open, is the author's semi-permeable wall; it is a knob of the protocol and is declared before a run like any other.
- **T5 at λ = 1.** Tempering at N = 160 with the full Hamiltonian gives a smooth curve from φ = 0.51 to 1.01 with no jump anywhere, passing through the digitised points of [KTB19] Fig. 8a to 0.005 for g ≥ 4. [T25] Fig. 3 is still not reproduced, rms 0.30 in this range. The reproduction stage's one outstanding disagreement is unchanged by better sampling.
- **What follows for the plan:** nothing changes in steps 2 to 4 or in the gates. Step 5 is added. Its direction A (look for such dips at simulable sizes, 1 < λ < 2) needs no new knob and so leaves S1 untouched; any direction that adds a knob needs its own dated decision here first.

Update 13 (2026-09-22): the vision audited against T6, T7 and the first sealed runs, and three vaguenesses settled by the author. Why this page changes: after T7 the project had a mechanism with the shape of claim 4 and no stated bar that applied to it, and two directional ambiguities that decide which track is the vision's. Details: `ASSUMPTIONS.md` O13 (T7), O14 (T9, in progress); `PREREGISTRATION.md` T7, T9, T10.

- **Direction, decided: X is curled, and the change uncurls.** X has fewer large dimensions than spacetime, not more. The illustration under "What X is" is revised in place. The λ > 1 track — a tube opening into a sheet — is therefore the vision's track and not merely the reachable one; the λ < 1 direction, where folding pays and the end state is shattered cubes, is the wrong way round and stays what Update 8 called it, a rehearsal.
- **Trigger, decided: the rule is fixed and the activation is supplied.** X is metastable at a fixed λ > 1 and goes over the wall when something pays the activation cost: a thermal fluctuation (T7) or a delivered push (the 12-unit spark of Update 9 and T9; the author's colliding black holes in the parked extension). λ does not run across 1 in the vision. Consequence: a sweep of λ across 1 is not a test of the hypothesis's trigger and drops in priority; the λ map (PREREGISTRATION T8) keeps its narrower purpose of locating where the metastable window closes, which O13 puts between 1.25 and 1.5 at g = 1.5.
- **The bar, decided: S2′**, written under "What success means" with its caveat beside it.
- **The tube is this model's stand-in for X.** Update 5's sentence that nothing simulated fits the author's definition of X was true when written and is out of date: the tube is a specific, relatively stable arrangement with its own dimensions, above spacetime in F. A stand-in, not X; O13's "not claimed: that X is a tube" stands. *(Annotated 2026-10-19, consistency audit: superseded as the stand-in by the curled slab, two directions open and one curled (Updates 47, 58, 60, 64); the tube stays the two-direction case of paper 1.)*
- **The claims, plainly, at this date.** Claim 4: for the disorder → order route the change is not sharp (T6, agreeing with the published work); for the order → order route it is sharp in every pre-registered sense (T7) and releases the exact gap, then does it again off a ledge that is one unconverted ring. Claim 5: the leftover is that ring. Sealed (T9, N = 64 and 96 read at this writing, N = 192 running), the verdict as written is BONFIRE WITH A THRESHOLD: complete conversion to a sheet when the bath can hold the lump, boil-off when it cannot, the crossover between N/4 and N/2 at both sizes with the predicted N/3.5 inside that interval, no stall anywhere, and a leftover ring in 83 to 100 % of the sheets. Claim 6: conserved to the last unit in every sealed run. Claims 1 and 3: the stand-in fits. Claim 2 and the spot: untouched.
- **What is falsifiable from here.** Whether the leftover grows with the space (T10, pre-registered before its runs). If it does not — one ring however large the tube — claim 5's leftover is a single defect and negligible at scale, and the claim is back to bookkeeping.
- **Task numbering.** PREREGISTRATION's T7, T8 and T9 are not TASKS.md's; the map is at the top of TASKS.md.
- **The author's extension of the same day** (tube opens at the Big Bang; gravity as the wish to refold; black holes as re-curled regions; colliding black holes seeding a new space) is parked under rule 2, with the two pieces the model can reach marked as step-4 items: `docs/parked/extension_2026-09-22.md`.

Update 14 (2026-09-22, later the same day): two rule decisions made by the author, and what each returned. Why this page changes: Update 13 left two pre-registered verdicts withheld by rules that had failed on positive cases; both are now decided. Details: `PREREGISTRATION.md` T6 amendment 4 and T7 amendment 2; `ASSUMPTIONS.md` O11 and O13 addenda.

- **λ = 0 is FIRST ORDER by the pre-registered verdict**, under amendment 4 (criterion 3 compared to the two-spike prediction, adopted by the author). A lump of 12.5 per point, a barrier growing at 34 standard errors, the Binder minimum 0.020 from its own two-spike value and closing. S2 is met at the penalty-off end of the knob — where, as Update 6 said, the cold phase is knots and not a space. Recorded with it: the rule was adopted after the data it changes the verdict on; the control, not the test cases, exposed the defect.
- **The tube → sheet verdict stays withheld, and now for a stated reason.** Under amendment 2 in its strict form (the energy checked at the sheet or at the four-point ledge, both exact), every decay that reached either passes; 14 of 240 reached neither by the 30,000-sweep cap. The author chose to replay those fourteen with the cap at 100,000 (amendment 3, enacted): all fourteen reproduced their originals, two finished to the sheet, and twelve rest on states that are neither the sheet nor the four-point remnant — six of them with their energy unchanged to the last digit over the extra 70,000 sweeps. So the decay has a family of resting states on the way down, the four-point remnant the common one and these the rare ones; gate 3's list of allowed states was an assumption the data outgrew. All three predictions hold at every size in both runs. What to do next is proposed amendment 4 — read the twelve states from the graph and check each against its own exact energy, or gate only the decays that reached the full release — and is the author's call. *(An earlier version of this bullet said the fourteen were "still moving"; that inference was wrong for at least six, and is corrected in O13.)*
- **λ ≥ 1 out of the random phase: INCONCLUSIVE by the letter, with the bound stated.** The reruns meet the round-trip gate everywhere; one hump at every λ, size and replica in the region where the network changes; any lump below 1.3 per point at N = 100 and falling — eight times smaller than the λ = 0 lump. Criterion 3 cannot be read there: the Binder energy cumulant depends on where zero energy sits, and at λ ≥ 1 the settled sheet *is* zero. A second criterion mis-specified, a second repair proposed (T6 amendment 5), the author's call again.
- **T9 is complete: BONFIRE WITH A THRESHOLD at all three sizes.** And the leftover in the coldest box is one ring at every size, which makes T10's committed prediction that it grows with the space likely to fail; O14 says so before T10 finishes. Whether the one ring is a seam is pre-registered as T11 and running.

Update 15 (2026-09-22, evening): the two withheld verdicts, under the rules the author chose. Why this page changes: Update 14 left both on hold pending her decisions; both are now made and run. Details: `PREREGISTRATION.md` T6 amendment 5 and T7 amendment 4; `ASSUMPTIONS.md` O17 and O13 addenda.

- **The tube → sheet change: TWO-STATE CHANGE** at N = 64, 96 and 192 (T7 amendment 4 (a): the twelve unusual resting states read from their wiring; eleven match their energy exactly). N = 144 is set aside on one decay that changed state inside its last measuring window, so its recorded release does not match its final structure; the three sharpness predictions hold there too. The rare resting states are combinations of small defects in the sheet, 8 to 45 units each, fixed in size — more of claim 5's leftover, not a larger one. **This is the verdict the vision's own route needed, in the sense the pre-registration defined.** S2′ is not thereby cleared, for the reason under S2′.
- **Out of the random phase at λ ≥ 1: still INCONCLUSIVE by the letter**, and the reason is the wording of amendment 5 (a), drafted by the assistant: the cumulant of φ has no dip at the transition, so its minimum always falls at the hot edge of the scan. One hump everywhere and the bound below 1.3 per point stand. The author decided the same evening to leave it there: INCONCLUSIVE is the final verdict of these runs, because the random-phase route is not the hypothesis's question and the measurements are the result whatever the label.

Update 16 (2026-09-22, late evening): a hypothesis of the author's about claim 5, recorded. Why this page changes: T10 showed the leftover is small and fixed in size, and the author decided that a small leftover is acceptable (the lump carrying the energy is what claim 4 needs) and proposed what the leftover is.

- **The author's words, put in order.** Dark matter is the leftover: the part of X that did not convert. Ordinary matter comes from the lump; dark matter is the latent remainder, made in the same change at the same moment. It does not interact with ordinary matter in the usual ways because it is a different kind of thing from what ordinary matter is made of, but it carries energy and so feels gravity. It might be abundant through many seeds, each leaving a scrap, or as a quality of spacetime itself. *(Annotated 2026-10-19, consistency audit: replaced by a direction's release in Update 30 (17:50 ET); open again beside it in Update 41; the order-of-openings story set aside in Update 50; the scrap is her lean as best guess in Update 52, which records that the stubborn knots hold far more than birth can afford.)*
- **Its published cousin** is [T24]'s allotropes: dark matter as patches of the network stuck in a different discrete arrangement. Claim 5 already names allotropes as a candidate for X2 (Update 10).
- **What a first look says** (`ASSUMPTIONS.md` O18, exploratory): a small leftover may be exactly what dark matter needs, because matter thins out more slowly than radiation — at the conversion the leftover need hold only about 0.7 eV divided by the starting temperature, as a share of the energy. So the many-seed route needs few seeds, and ties their density to how hot the universe began. The "quality of spacetime" route, energy kept spread through the geometry, would overshoot, and in the sealed runs a geometry holding a large share of the energy is one whose sheet has melted.
- **The test** is TASKS T14 (leftover per seed), now with a second question: how the leftover scales with the number of seeds. Nothing here is a result; it is a hypothesis with a stated way to be wrong.

Update 17 (2026-09-22, night): a hypothesis of the author's about the quantum half of claim 2, recorded. Why this page changes: claim 2 names quantum mechanics as something X must give back, and until now the project had nothing to say about it. The author proposed a mechanism, it rests on a knob this page already decided (Update 12, interchangeable points), and the model already holds an exact count that bears on it (ASSUMPTIONS Q15).

- **The author's words, put in order.** Points that can be swapped without changing any relationship are not two points: a version of the network with them renamed is the same reality, and from inside a loop with no start, no end and no outside there is no vantage point from which the versions could be told apart, so all of them hold at once. That "all at once" is the wave: superposition is the set of undetectably different versions of the world, all standing, with the most probable one being what you would get if forced to pick. Measurement is an interaction that adds a relationship a swap would not preserve; the versions now differ and you are locked into one. Time playing out does the same, one interaction at a time. A particle is an allowed vibration of a small closed loop of the substrate, and the frequency sets its type. Because the loop is eternal, every version of every degree of freedom that can come out of the lump does come out, so "every version exists" is literal, and interaction is what separates them.
- **What it is in standard language** (*ours; general knowledge, none of it read by us, all to verify before it is cited*). (a) The exchange symmetry of identical particles: quantum mechanics holds every assignment of indistinguishable particles at once because no experiment can tell them apart, which is her first step in the textbook. (b) Quantised modes on a closed loop: only whole numbers of waves fit, so frequencies are discrete (standing waves; Kaluza–Klein charges as waves round a curled direction; string theory's particle types as vibration modes). (c) Measurement as an interaction that makes versions distinguishable: decoherence (Zurek) and relational quantum mechanics (Rovelli, 1996). (d) Bell's theorem constrains local hidden facts; the substrate has no locality, so the premise does not apply as stated, **but nothing here shows that the picture reproduces the correlations Bell's experiments measure, and that is the real bar.**
- **What the model already holds, exact.** Q15's renaming counts at N = 160: a melted graph 1, a perfect sheet 320, a shattered state about 10^29.4; and the exact labelled against unlabelled ensembles at N = 16 and 18 (TASKS T10). Under her reading a fully interacting melted region has one version and is completely "measured", while isolated ordered loops keep their versions and are wavelike, which is the right way round for a world that looks classical at large scale. Also exact (Laplacian spectrum, computed 2026-09-22): a closed loop of four points has non-zero frequencies √2 (twice: the two ways round) and 2; a loop of six has 1 (twice), √3 (twice) and 2.
- **The strain, said once** (*ours*). [T25] calls the melted phase matter, and the melted phase has exactly one version. So in this reading the wavelike object is a small closed loop, not the melt, and which object plays the particle has to be decided before any test is designed. The leftover of T10 (a four-point remnant lying along the tube, O16; and the rarer 20-unit twist, O15) is the natural candidate and has not been read for either its symmetry or its spectrum.
- **What would test it, all exact and none needing a new knob** (TASKS T13, rung 4): (i) the renaming count of the saved T10 leftovers; (ii) their Laplacian spectra, to see whether the two leftover types have distinct frequencies; (iii) whether an ordered region's renaming count drops to 1 when it is joined to a melted one, which is the measurement step; (iv) the bar that matters, Bell-type correlations between two measurements, which needs a notion of a measurement in the model that does not exist yet.
- **Status:** a hypothesis with a stated way to be wrong. Not on the scorecard. Written into the public document `docs/public/the-loop-and-the-floor_v1.html` as its Part V, which by that document's own rule is told without hedges.

Update 18 (2026-09-22, night): the model's author replied. Why this page changes: three statements on this page rest on readings of his papers that his reply disputes or moves, and S5 has a status for the first time. His email is private and is paraphrased in `docs/outreach/correspondence_2026-09-22_trugenberger.md`; it is not to be quoted publicly.

- **The λ → ∞ end of the knob is not a published point.** Update 3 read [KTB19] Sec. 4 as running its simulations on the space with no edge above d − 2 squares, and called the capped kernel "a published case". The author says he has no such restriction in his code and that only the hard-core rule is ever imposed. So the knob has **two** published points, λ = 0 and λ = 1, and the cap is our reading of one sentence, now disputed by its author. The capped results stay on the record as the λ → ∞ end; they are not a reproduction of anything. The kernel that is his model is λ = 1 without the cap, which is the one that matches Fig. 8a to rms 0.005.
- **His current position on the order of the transition is "hybrid", not "continuous".** From his own runs at N = 1024 with the review's code and a newer one: two continuous branches with a jump between them, which he calls typical of network models. The root question's sentence that the published evidence for "continuous" is one correlation-length plot stands as written, and it is now joined by the author's own later view that there is a jump. Whether that jump is equilibrium (present at the same coupling in both directions) or the lattice branch surviving past the transition on cold ascent with a short warm-up is not known, and it is the next pre-registered run: T6 at N = 4p², both directions, with tempering. **This is the first movement in the disorder → order question since T6's verdict, and it comes from the author, not from us.**
- **Sizes: N = 4p², p prime, for every disorder → order run from here**, on his advice that the ground state is unique there. The tube track keeps its own sizes (a 4 × L torus needs L even).
- **S5: the door is open, not passed.** He read the note, could not picture the tube from words, and asked what we meant. He has not seen the plots or the code. The public pages say nothing more than that.

Update 19 (2026-09-23, morning): the author decided what matter is in this reading; the first exact test of Update 17 was pre-registered with her predictions and run; the model's author wrote again; and the newest explanation of Gate B was checked and does not fit. Why this page changes: Update 17 recorded a strain against itself — [T25] calls the melt matter, and the melt has exactly one version — and said the object that plays the particle had to be decided before any test could be designed. It was decided, the test ran, and its result restates Update 17 in a form the author has to rule on. Details: `PREREGISTRATION.md` T15 rung 0; `ASSUMPTIONS.md` O27 and O28; `docs/outreach/correspondence_2026-09-22_trugenberger.md`, second note.

- **Decision (the author's, 23 September): matter is the structured leftover, not the melt.** Her words: "why call melted phase matter? That's not matter." Her reasons, put in order: matter has species, conserved quantities and discrete masses, and a maximum-entropy tangle offers none of them; the leftovers this project has actually measured are structured defects of fixed size, 8 to 45 units (O13, O15, O16), not blobs of melt; and [T24] itself moves the same way, making dark matter allotropes rather than melt. The strain in Update 17 is resolved by this decision. The comparison table's row on where matter comes from is annotated accordingly; what [T25] calls matter is left as his.
- **T15 rung 0, exact, pre-registered with her predictions and run the same morning: INCONCLUSIVE by the letter** (O28). A melt has exactly one version at every size, as predicted. A sheet with a defect in it has more than one version on the isolated count in every case, up to 36,864, and on the whole-world count in 10 of 11 states; the eleventh, a world with three different defects in it, has exactly one. The versions of the whole turn out to be the sheet's own symmetries surviving where the defects happen to sit, not anything the object carries. The leftover types, read from their wiring, are a closed loop of four (the four-point remnant, which is the object this Update 17 named before it had been read), the 3-cube, an open line of three, single edges and single points, with pairwise distinct spectra; the letter's failure on the frequency prediction is between names that differ only in local dimension while being the same graph. Two post-hoc readings under which every prediction holds — per remnant type, and type by wiring — are on the record as proposed, not enacted.
- **What this says about Update 17, plainly** (*ours, unverified*). "Superposition is the set of undetectably different versions of the world" survives only for symmetric worlds: the first world with three different things in it has one version, as the melt does. What survives is the object-level statement, that an isolated small loop has versions and that its spectrum labels its structure. Whether Update 17 is restated at the object level, read per remnant type, or left as it stands is the author's call. Rung 1, whether the counts are what the loop's time-fractions equal, is untouched and is still the rung that would make any of this a probability.
- **The author's second note, and Gate B** (addendum to Update 18; O27). He wrote, unprompted, that his procedure of starting each coupling from the previous coupling's final graph may accentuate the apparent jump by trapping configurations in the wrong phase, and that a diverging correlation length points to a continuous or mixed transition. That is T13's reading (b), named by the person best placed to know, and our N = 196 runs already show it: under his protocol the lattice survives past the transition on ascent and collapses in three chains of four, while tempering from a melt gives one smooth curve. As an explanation of [T25] Fig. 3 it does not fit: Fig. 3 sits above Fig. 8a in both directions, across the whole transition, with its crossover at a larger coupling, where trapping on descent would put a curve *below* equilibrium and our own descent under his protocol shows no departure at all. Gate B stays open, and the question for him narrows to the coupling axis or the size of Fig. 3. On the disorder → order route this strengthens "continuous", which is the published question's answer and not, by Update 13, the hypothesis's; the tube route is untouched.

Update 20 (2026-09-23, later the same morning): the quantum leg, as stated in Update 17, cannot violate Bell, and this was found by argument before anything was built. Why this page changes: the author asked why the model could not be tested against a quantum prediction directly, the GHZ test of the design brief was pre-registered with her prediction, and working through its definitions settled it for every possible arrangement. Details: `PREREGISTRATION.md` T15 rung 3a and its reading; `ASSUMPTIONS.md` O29.

- **The argument, in one sentence.** A renaming of a fixed arrangement fixes or swaps each loop's two pairs of points at once, so it is a complete instruction set: the outcomes of every setting of every loop exist together before any measurement, attaching a detector only decides which renamings survive and never which colours they give, and Bell's theorem applies. GHZ is CLASSICAL and CHSH gives S ≤ 2 for any arrangement. **Her prediction (strongly contextual) fails.** *(Ours, unverified: a short argument, unreviewed by a physicist.)*
- **What survives of Update 17.** The object-level counting: a small closed loop has versions, its spectrum labels its structure, and rungs 0 to 2 test that counting and not Bell. Interference, rung 4, which was always going to need a dynamical rule this page has not decided. And the "no vantage point inside the loop" step, which is untouched as philosophy and unused as physics.
- **What does not survive.** That the versions of one fixed arrangement, read by a measurement, reproduce quantum correlations. The brief said in advance (section 5) that this outcome leaves the public document's Part V "a description of identical-particle symmetry and no more". Whether to restate Part V that way, or to reformulate the hypothesis over *histories* rather than arrangements, which is a different claim needing its own dated statement here, is the author's call.

Update 21 (2026-09-23, afternoon): the author asked how else the quantum predictions could be tested; the reading and the proposal are in `docs/design/amplitude_rule_brief.md`; she chose to hold the proposed wave rule until one exact question had been answered, and it has been. Why this page changes: Update 17's sentence "a particle is an allowed vibration of a small closed loop" has now been tested against the model's own loop in its own sheet, and it fails as stated. Details: `PREREGISTRATION.md` T15 rung 0b; `ASSUMPTIONS.md` O30; `REFERENCES.bib` [Hardy01], [Gorard20].

- **The gap, named.** Between counting versions and quantum mechanics there is exactly one ingredient, and it has a name: Hardy's fifth axiom, a continuous reversible transformation between pure states. Discrete renamings do not have it; a vibration does. The exact bridge is that a loop's vibrations are the Fourier modes of its versions, so the author's identification of *what* is in superposition stands; the rule for *how* versions combine is what was missing, and the nearest published programme ([Gorard20]) puts it in by hand and meets Bell by non-locality.
